The USMC F-35B [Fleet Replacement Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleet_Replacement_Squadron) (FRS) was initially based at Eglin AFB in 2012 alongside USAF F-35A training units, before moving to [MCAS Beaufort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MCAS_Beaufort) in 2014; another FRS was stood up at [MCAS Miramar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MCAS_Miramar) in 2020.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] The USAF holds its basic F-35A course at Eglin AFB and Luke AFB; in January 2013, training began at Eglin with capacity for 100 pilots and 2,100 maintainers at once.[^cite_note-pilot_training-306] The [6th Weapons Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_Weapons_Squadron) of the [USAF Weapons School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USAF_Weapons_School) was activated at [Nellis AFB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nellis_AFB) in June 2017 for F-35A weapons instructor curriculum; the [65th Aggressor Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/65th_Aggressor_Squadron) was reactivated with the F-35A in June 2022 to expand training against adversary stealth aircraft tactics.[^cite_note-307] The USN stood up its F-35C FRS in 2012—[VFA-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-101) at Eglin AFB—then in 2019 consolidated operations under [VFA-125](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-125) at [NAS Lemoore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAS_Lemoore) in 2019.[^cite_note-308] The F-35C was introduced to the Strike Fighter Tactics Instructor course, or [TOPGUN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TOPGUN), in 2020, with the course syllabus revamped to accommodate the aircraft's additional capabilities.[^cite_note-309]

#### US Marine Corps

On 16 November 2012, the USMC received the first F-35B of [VMFA-121](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VMFA-121) at [MCAS Yuma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Corps_Air_Station_Yuma).[^cite_note-310] The USMC declared Initial Operational Capability (IOC) for the F-35B in the Block 2B configuration on 31 July 2015 after operational trials, with some limitations in night operations, mission systems, and weapons carriage.[^cite_note-USMC_IOC-2][^cite_note-311] USMC F-35Bs participated in their first Red Flag exercise in July 2016 with 67 sorties conducted.[^cite_note-312] The first F-35B deployment occurred in 2017 at MCAS Iwakuni, Japan; combat employment began in July 2018 from the amphibious assault ship [USS *Essex*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Essex_\(LHD-2\)), with the first combat strike on 27 September 2018 against a [Taliban](30635.md) target in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-313]

During a conflict, the USMC plans to disperse the aircraft among austere forward-deployed bases with shelter and concealment to improve survivability while remaining close to a battlespace. Known as distributed STOVL operations (DSO), F-35Bs would operate from temporary bases in allied territory within hostile missile engagement zones and displace inside the enemy's 24- to 48-hour targeting cycle; this strategy allows F-35Bs to rapidly respond to operational needs, with mobile forward arming and refueling points (M-FARPs) accommodating [KC-130](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_KC-130) and [MV-22 Osprey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MV-22_Osprey) aircraft to rearm and refuel the jets, as well as littoral areas for sea links of mobile distribution sites. For higher echelons of maintenance, F-35Bs would return from M-FARPs to rear-area friendly bases or ships. Helicopter-portable metal planking is needed to protect unprepared roads from the F-35B's exhaust; the USMC is studying lighter heat-resistant options.[^cite_note-314] These operations have become part of the larger USMC Expeditionary Advanced Base Operations (EABO) concept.[^cite_note-315]

The first USMC F-35C squadron, [VMFA-314](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VMFA-314), achieved [full operational capability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_operational_capability) in July 2021 and was first deployed on board USS *Abraham Lincoln* as a part of Carrier Air Wing 9 in January 2022.[^cite_note-316]

In 2024, Lt. Gen. Sami Sadat of Afghanistan described an operation in which F-35Bs from [USS *Essex*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Essex_\(LHD-2\)) bombed a Taliban position through cloud cover. "The impact \[the F-35] left on my soldiers was amazing. Like, whoa, you know, we have this technology", Sadat said. "But also the impact on the Taliban was quite crippling, because they have never seen Afghan forces move in the winter, and they have never seen planes that could bomb through the clouds."[^cite_note-317]

On 9 November 2024, Marine F-35Cs struck targets associated with the [Houthi movement](17240332.md) in [Yemen](350939.md) during the [Red Sea crisis](75207766.md)—possibly the first time the F-35C was used in combat.[^cite_note-Trevithickyemen-318]

#### US Air Force

USAF F-35As first achieved IOC in 2016: aircraft in the Block 3i configuration with the [34th Fighter Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/34th_Fighter_Squadron) at [Hill Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_Air_Force_Base), [Utah](31716.md), on 2 August.[^cite_note-defready_aug16-3] F-35As conducted their first Red Flag exercise the following year; system maturity had improved and the aircraft scored a kill ratio of 15\:1 against an F-16 [aggressor squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aggressor_squadron) in a high-threat environment.[^cite_note-319] F-35As were first deployed on 15 April 2019 to [Al Dhafra Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Dhafra_Air_Base), UAE;[^cite_note-320] 12 days later, they were first used in combat—in an airstrike on an [Islamic State](9087364.md) tunnel network in northern Iraq.[^cite_note-usaf20190427-321]

F-35As were first stationed in Europe in 2021, when two squadrons totaling 48 aircraft were added to the [48th Fighter Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/48th_Fighter_Wing)'s [F-15C](11715.md) and [F-15E](495724.md) squadrons at [RAF Lakenheath](856907.md) in the UK. The first aircraft of the 495th Fighter Squadron arrived on 15 December 2021.[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-The_Aviationist-323]

The F-35's operating cost is higher than some older USAF tactical aircraft. In 2015, the cost per flight hour (CPFH) of the A-10 was \$17,716; the F-15C, \$41,921; and the F-16C, \$22,514.[^cite_note-324] In fiscal year 2018, the F-35A's CPFH was \$44,000; this shrank to \$35,000 in 2019,[^cite_note-325] and Lockheed Martin said it hoped to reduce it to \$25,000 by 2025 through performance-based logistics and other measures.[^cite_note-326]

In 2025, US officials said F-35As and [F-22s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-22_Raptor) launched from land bases in the Persian Gulf region were used to attempt to draw surface-to-air missile fire ahead of [B-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_B-2_Spirit) bombing runs during the [United States strikes on Iranian nuclear sites](80262844.md).[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329]

In January 2026, F-35s participated in the [United States strikes in Venezuela](82021123.md), along with [F-22s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-22_Raptor), [B-1s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B-1_Lancer), [F/A-18s](845012.md), [EA-18s](833647.md), and various intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance aircraft and drones. General [Dan Caine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Caine) claimed the aircraft were used to disable Venezuelan air defense systems to ensure the safe passage of the helicopters into the target area. The operation resulted in the capture of Venezuelan President [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md).[^cite_note-330]

#### US Navy



VFA-147 F-35C catches the wire on [USS *George Washington*](198834.md)

The USN achieved operational status with the F-35C in Block 3F on 28 February 2019.[^cite_note-\:0-4] On 2 August 2021, the F-35C of [VFA-147](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VFA-147), as well as the [CMV-22 Osprey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_Boeing_V-22_Osprey), embarked on their maiden deployments as part of Carrier Air Wing 2 on board [USS *Carl Vinson*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Carl_Vinson).[^cite_note-331]

USN F-35Cs operating from the USS *Carl Vinson* took part in the training exercise Pacific Stellar 2025 in February, along with the French and Japanese navies.[^cite_note-332]

In April 2025, F-35Cs from VFA-97 shot down multiple Houthi drones over the Red Sea, making it the first time the Navy has used the variant in combat.[^cite_note-333]

### United Kingdom



F-35B *ZM148* of [No. 617 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._617_Squadron_RAF) landing on HMS *Queen Elizabeth*, 2019

The United Kingdom's [Royal Air Force](25679.md) and Royal Navy operate the F-35B. Called Lightning in British service,[^cite_note-334] it has replaced the [Harrier GR9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Aerospace_Harrier_II), retired in 2010, and [Tornado GR4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panavia_Tornado), retired in 2019. The F-35 is to be Britain's primary strike aircraft for the next three decades. One of the Royal Navy's requirements was a [Shipborne Rolling and Vertical Landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SRVL) (SRVL) mode to increase maximum landing weight by using wing lift during landing.[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336] Like the Italian Navy, British F-35Bs use [ski-jumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ski-jump_ramp) to fly from their aircraft carriers, [HMS *Queen Elizabeth*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Queen_Elizabeth_\(R08\)) and [HMS *Prince of Wales*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Prince_of_Wales_\(R09\)). British F-35Bs are not intended to use the Brimstone 2 missile.[^cite_note-337] In July 2013, [Chief of the Air Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Air_Staff_\(United_Kingdom\)) Air Chief Marshal [Sir Stephen Dalton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Stephen_Dalton) announced that [No. 617 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._617_Squadron_RAF) would be the RAF's first operational F-35 squadron.[^cite_note-at19j13-338][^cite_note-gov18j13-339]

The first British F-35 squadron was [No. 17 (Reserve) Test and Evaluation Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._17_Squadron_RAF) (TES), which stood up on 12 April 2013 as the aircraft's [Operational Evaluation Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operational_Evaluation_Unit).[^cite_note-340] By June 2013, the RAF had received three F-35s of the 48 on order, initially based at Eglin Air Force Base.[^cite_note-341] In June 2015, the F-35B undertook its first launch from a ski-jump at NAS Patuxent River.[^cite_note-342] On 5 July 2017, it was announced that the second UK-based RAF squadron would be [No. 207 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._207_Squadron_RAF),[^cite_note-343] which reformed on 1 August 2019 as the Lightning [Operational Conversion Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operational_Conversion_Unit) (OCU).[^cite_note-344] No. 617 Squadron reformed on 18 April 2018 during a ceremony in Washington, D.C., becoming the first RAF front-line squadron to operate the type;[^cite_note-345] receiving its first four F-35Bs on 6 June, flying from MCAS Beaufort to [RAF Marham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Marham).[^cite_note-346] On 10 January 2019, No. 617 Squadron and its F-35s were declared combat-ready.[^cite_note-347]

April 2019 saw the first overseas deployment of a UK F-35 squadron when No. 617 Squadron went to [RAF Akrotiri](432784.md), [Cyprus](5593.md).[^cite_note-348] This reportedly led on 25 June 2019 to the first combat use of an RAF F-35B: an armed reconnaissance flight searching for Islamic State targets in Iraq and Syria.[^cite_note-349] In October 2019, F-35s of 617 Squadron and No. 17 TES were embarked on HMS *Queen Elizabeth* for the first time.[^cite_note-350] No. 617 Squadron departed RAF Marham on 22 January 2020 for their first [Exercise Red Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exercise_Red_Flag) with the Lightning.[^cite_note-351] As of November 2022, 26 F-35Bs were based in the United Kingdom (with 617 and 207 Squadrons) and a further three were permanently based in the United States (with 17 Squadron) for testing and evaluation purposes.[^cite_note-auto4-352]

The UK's second operational squadron is the [Fleet Air Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleet_Air_Arm)'s [809 Naval Air Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/809_Naval_Air_Squadron), which stood up in December 2023.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-Royal_Navy-354][^cite_note-355]

In June 2025, as part of its second phase of procurement, the UK announced plans to procure 12 F-35As, capable of delivering both conventional and nuclear weapons, including the [B61-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B61_nuclear_bomb#B61_Mod_12) thermonuclear gravity bomb. The aircraft will form part of NATO's [dual-capable aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_sharing#NATO) program. The F-35As will be based in RAF Marham to be used in a training role on routine operations as part of the OCU. 15 F-35Bs are also planned in this phase.[^cite_note-\:10-356][^cite_note-357][^cite_note-\:11-358]

In July 2025, United States B61 nuclear weapons were redeployed to the US-operated [RAF Lakenheath](856907.md) for the first time since 2008. The base operates F-35As and [F-15Es](495724.md), both capable of delivering the B61.[^cite_note-y1942-359][^cite_note-360]

On March 3, 2026, the Ministry of Defence confirmed that a British F-35B shot down a hostile drone over Jordan, marking the first time a British F-35 has destroyed a target in operations.[^cite_note-361]

### Australia



Four F-35As assigned to [No. 3 Squadron RAAF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No._3_Squadron_RAAF) in 2023

Australia's first F-35, designated A35-001, was manufactured in 2014, with flight training provided through the international Pilot Training Center (PTC) at Luke Air Force Base in Arizona.[^cite_note-\:5-362] The first two F-35s were unveiled to the Australian public on 3 March 2017 at the [Avalon Airshow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_International_Airshow).[^cite_note-\:6-363] By 2021, the [Royal Australian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force) had accepted 26 F-35As, with nine in the US and 17 operating at No 3 Squadron and No 2 Operational Conversion Unit at [RAAF Base Williamtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAAF_Base_Williamtown).[^cite_note-\:5-362] With 41 trained RAAF pilots and 225 trained technicians for maintenance, the fleet was declared ready to deploy on operations.[^cite_note-364] It was originally expected that Australia would receive all 72 F-35s by 2023.[^cite_note-\:6-363] Its final nine aircraft, which were the TR-3 version, arrived in Australia in December 2024.[^cite_note-DefenceJSFDec2024-365]

### Israel

The Israeli Air Force (IAF) declared the F-35 operationally capable on 6 December 2017.[^cite_note-366] According to Kuwaiti newspaper *Al Jarida*, in July 2018, a test mission of at least three IAF F-35s flew to Iran's capital [Tehran](57654.md) and back to [Tel Aviv](31453.md). While publicly unconfirmed, regional leaders acted on the report; Iran's supreme leader [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) reportedly fired the air force chief and commander of Iran's [Revolutionary Guard Corps](5613577.md) over the mission.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]

On 22 May 2018, IAF chief [Amikam Norkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amikam_Norkin) said that the service had employed its F-35Is in two attacks on two battle fronts. This was part of the [May 2018 Israel-Iran clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2018_Israel-Iran_clashes), where airstrikes were claimed to target Iranian positions surrounding [Syria](7515849.md)'s capital of [Damascus](8914.md) and the buffer [UNDOF Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNDOF_Zone) of the [occupied Golan Heights](60364960.md).[^cite_note-369] This marks the first combat operation of an F-35 by any country.[^cite_note-first_strike-18][^cite_note-370] Norkin said it had been flown "all over the Middle East", and showed photos of an F-35I flying over [Beirut](37428.md) in daylight.[^cite_note-371] In July 2019, Israel expanded its strikes against Iranian missile shipments; IAF F-35Is allegedly struck Iranian targets in Iraq twice.[^cite_note-372]

In November 2020, the IAF announced that a recent four-plane delivery included a unique F-35I [testbed aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testbed_aircraft) to be used to test and integrate Israeli-produced weapons and electronic systems on later F-35s. It is the only testbed F-35 delivered to a non-US customer.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-IsraelF35i-374]

On 11 May 2021, eight IAF F-35Is took part in an attack on 150 targets in [Hamas](13913.md)' rocket array, including 50 to 70 launch pits in the northern [Gaza Strip](12047.md), as part of [Operation Guardian of the Walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Guardian_of_the_Walls).[^cite_note-375] On 6 March 2022, the IDF stated that on 15 March 2021, F-35Is shot down two Iranian [drones](58900.md) carrying weapons to the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-376] This was the first operational interception and downing carried out by the F-35. They were also used in the [Gaza war](74998519.md).[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379] On 2 November 2023, the IDF posted on social media that they used an F-35I to shoot down a [Houthi](17240332.md) cruise missile over the Red Sea that was fired from Yemen during the Gaza war.[^cite_note-380]

F-35Is were used in airstrikes against Lebanon, including aerial refueling in the country's airspace, in August and September 2024, as part of the [Israel–Hezbollah conflict (2023–present)](75005425.md).[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-auto6-176] F-35Is were used in the [29 September 2024 Israeli attacks on Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/29_September_2024_Israeli_attacks_on_Yemen).[^cite_note-382] F-35Is were also reportedly involved in the [October 2024 Israeli strikes on Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2024_Israeli_strikes_on_Iran).[^cite_note-383] The F-35 was also used during the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War), equipped with low-observable [conformal fuel tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conformal_fuel_tank) to extend their range and allow operations over Iranian soil without mid-air refueling.[^cite_note-auto2-384] Iranian state-run media claimed that Iran shot down several Israeli F-35 jets, though the IDF denied the claims.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386]

From 28 February 2026, Israeli F-35s participated in [US-Israeli strikes on Iran](82533582.md#Iran), beginning the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md). About 200 IAF jets, including F-35Is and [F-15s](11715.md), struck about 500 targets. The US had also sent more F-35s to the region during [the prewar buildup](82251337.md).[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388] On 4 March, the IDF announced its F-35I shot down an [Islamic Republic of Iran Air Force](412792.md) [Yakovlev Yak-130](1513240.md) combat trainer over Tehran; the first claimed air-to-air kill of a crewed aircraft by any F-35, and the first IAF air-to-air kill of a crewed aircraft since November 1985, when an F-15 downed a pair of Syrian MiG-23s over Lebanon. According to the IAF, the engagement was conducted at beyond visual range using long-range missiles, and was described as a "rapid response" rather than a close-range dogfight. The Yak-130 was reportedly flying a counter-drone mission over the Iranian capital at the time. Unverified footage appeared to show the Yak-130 going down in a mountainous area north of Tehran, with two ejections visible.[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392]

The Israeli Air Force claimed F-35I wartime [combat readiness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combat_readiness) rates of 90%, while a study by the [United States Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Government_Accountability_Office) showed a decline in combat readiness of American F-35s, dropping from 67% in 2021 to 44% in 2025. The IAF has used the F-35 frequently during recent conflicts, including the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md), and it has used multiple tiers of IAF staff who maintain the aircraft.[^cite_note-393]

### Italy

Italy's F-35As were declared to have reached initial operational capability (IOC) on 30 November 2018. At the time, Italy had taken delivery of 10 F-35As and one F-35B, with 2 F-35As and the one F-35B being stationed in the US for training; the remaining 8 F-35As were stationed in Amendola.[^cite_note-394] [Italian Navy](1126939.md) F-35Bs have been operating from the Italian aircraft carrier [ITS *Cavour*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_aircraft_carrier_Cavour), where they have also conducted drills in the [Philippine Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Sea) with the US in 2024.[^cite_note-\:8-395][^cite_note-396] Construction in 2023 showed that [Ghedi Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghedi_Air_Base) is planning to host F-35As for Italian delivery of US B61 nuclear weapons under NATO [nuclear sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_sharing).[^cite_note-397]

### Japan



F-35A in afterburner at JASDF [Misawa Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misawa_Air_Base)

Japan's F-35As were declared to have reached initial operational capability (IOC) on 29 March 2019. At the time Japan had taken delivery of 10 F-35As stationed in [Misawa Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misawa_Air_Base). Japan plans to eventually acquire a total of 147 F-35s, which will include 42 F-35Bs. It plans to use the latter variant to equip Japan's [*Izumo*-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izumo-class_multi-purpose_destroyer) multi-purpose destroyers.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399] On August 7, 2025, the first three Japanese F-35B's landed at [Nyutabaru Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyutabaru_Air_Base). Four more are to arrive by March 2026.[^cite_note-400]

On 9 April 2019, a JASDF F-35A (tail number 79-8705) attached to [Misawa Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misawa_Air_Base) crashed east of the [Aomori Prefecture](61732.md) during a training mission over the Pacific Ocean at an estimated speed of Mach 0.9.[^cite_note-spatial_disorientation-401] Japan grounded its 12 F-35As during the investigation. The US Navy and [Japan Maritime Self-Defense Force](2370744.md) searched for the missing aircraft and pilot, finding debris soon afterward[^cite_note-spatial_disorientation-401] and recovered the pilot's remains in June.[^cite_note-402] The aircraft [flight recorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_recorder) was too damaged for any data to be retrieved.[^cite_note-spatial_disorientation-401] Despite speculation that China or Russia might attempt to [salvage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvage_diving) the aircraft, the [Japanese Defense Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Defense_Ministry) reported no "reported activities" from either country.[^cite_note-\:1-403] The pilot had radioed his intention to abort the drill and was apparently conscious and responsive until 15 seconds before crashing, but he sent no [distress signal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distress_signal) nor attempted any recovery maneuvers as he descended at a rapid rate. The accident report attributed the cause to the pilot's [spatial disorientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spatial_disorientation).[^cite_note-spatial_disorientation-401] As of 2025, this is the only known fatal crash of any F-35 variant.[^cite_note-\:20-404]

### Norway



First Norwegian F-35A Lightning II at Luke Air Force Base, Arizona

On 6 November 2019, Norway declared initial operational capability (IOC) for its fleet of 15 F-35As out of a planned 52 F-35As.[^cite_note-405] On 6 January 2022, Norway's F-35As replaced its older F-16A and B models for the NATO quick reaction alert mission in the high north.[^cite_note-406] In April 2025, the total number of F-35s delivered totaled 49 out of 52.[^cite_note-\:2-407][^cite_note-408]

On 22 September 2023, two F-35As from the [Royal Norwegian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Norwegian_Air_Force) landed on a motorway near [Tervo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tervo), Finland, showing, for the first time, that F-35As can operate from paved roads. Unlike the F-35B, they cannot land vertically. The fighters were also refueled with their engines running. Major General Rolf Folland, Commander of the RNAF, said: "Fighter jets are vulnerable on the ground, so by being able to use small airfields – and now motorways – (this) increases our survivability in war".[^cite_note-409]

### Netherlands

On 27 December 2021, the Netherlands declared initial operational capability (IOC) for its fleet of 24 F-35As it has received to date from its order for 46 F-35As.[^cite_note-auto3-410] In 2022, the Netherlands announced it will order an additional six F-35s, totaling 52 aircraft ordered.[^cite_note-411] As of September 2024, 40 out of the 52 ordered have been delivered, and the Netherlands seeks to order another six jets to help completely phase out their F-16 fleet.[^cite_note-\:2-407] As of 2026, under [Dutch participation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) in NATO [nuclear sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_sharing), the F-35As at [Volkel Air Base](13925473.md) are assigned a nuclear mission, with US [B61 Mod 12 nuclear bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B61_nuclear_bomb) available to them,[^cite_note-fasUS2026-19] numbering 10 to 15 as of 2023.[^cite_note-fasShare2023-137]

## Variants

The F-35 was designed with three initial variants – the F-35A, a CTOL land-based version; the F-35B, a STOVL version capable of use either on land or on aircraft carriers; and the F-35C, a CATOBAR carrier-based version. Since then, there has been work on the design of nationally specific versions for Israel and Canada.

- The three main variants: CTOL for conventional take-off and landing, STOVL for short take-off and vertical-landing, and CV for carrier variant

  &#x20;The three main variants: [CTOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CTOL) for conventional take-off and landing, [STOVL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STOVL) for short take-off and vertical-landing, and CV for carrier variant

- USAF F-35A in flight, 2013

  &#x20;USAF F-35A in flight, 2013

- &#x20;Video of a USMC F-35B conducting the first vertical landing aboard USS *Wasp* on 3 October 2011

- F-35 variants flying in formation. The F-35C (left) has a larger wing than other variants, while the F-35B (center) has a shorter nozzle and no tailhook.

  &#x20;F-35 variants flying in formation. The F-35C (left) has a larger wing than other variants, while the F-35B (center) has a shorter nozzle and no tailhook.

### F-35A



[JASDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Air_Self-Defense_Force) F-35A Lightning II prepares to receive fuel from a USAF [KC-135 Stratotanker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KC-135_Stratotanker) in February 2025.

The F-35A is the [conventional take-off and landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conventional_take-off_and_landing) (CTOL) variant intended for the USAF and other air forces. It is the smallest, lightest version and capable of 9 [g](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-force), the highest of all variants.

Although the F-35A currently conducts [aerial refueling](237949.md) via the boom and receptacle method, the aircraft can be modified for [probe-and-drogue](237949.md#Probe-and-drogue) refueling if needed by the customer.[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413] A [drag chute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drogue_parachute) pod can be installed on the F-35A, with the [Royal Norwegian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Norwegian_Air_Force) being the first operator to adopt it.[^cite_note-auto-414] The F-35A has a [tailhook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailhook) designed to stop the aircraft during emergency situations. Unlike the more robust unit of the carrier-based F-35C, the F-35A's tailhook is a single-use device.[^cite_note-415]

### F-35B

See also: [Joint Combat Aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Combat_Aircraft)

The F-35B is the [short take-off and vertical landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_take-off_and_vertical_landing) (STOVL) variant of the aircraft. Similar in size to the A variant, the B sacrifices about a third of the A variant's fuel volume to accommodate the shaft-driven lift fan (SDLF).[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-From_Archive.org-2117330-417] This variant is limited to 7 g. Unlike other variants, the F-35B has no landing hook. The "STOVL/HOOK" control instead engages conversion between normal and vertical flight.[^cite_note-guyReac-418][^cite_note-419] The F-35B is capable of Mach 1.6 (1,960 km/h; 1,220 mph) and can perform vertical and/or short take-off and landing ([V/STOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V/STOL)).[^cite_note-lift1-243]

- F-35B cutaway with lift fan

  F-35B cutaway with lift fan

- US Marine Corps F-35B conducting a vertical landing on JS Izumo (note the rotated nozzle and the lift fan vent duct)

  US Marine Corps F-35B conducting a vertical landing on [JS *Izumo*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JS_Izumo) (note the rotated nozzle and the lift fan vent duct)

### F-35C



A [US Navy](20518076.md) F-35C conducting a landing on the [USS George Washington](198834.md).

The F-35C is the [carrier-based](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrier-based_aircraft) variant of the aircraft, designed for [catapult-assisted take-off and barrier-arrested recovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CATOBAR) (CATOBAR) operations from [aircraft carriers](2219.md). Compared to the F-35A, the F-35C incorporates several modifications to meet the specific demands of carrier operations. These include larger wings with [foldable wingtips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folding_wing) to reduce the aircraft's footprint for storage, enlarged control surfaces for improved handling at low speeds, a more robust tailhook for repeated use with carrier [arrestor cables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrestor_cable), a twin-wheel nose gear, and reinforced landing gear to withstand the stresses of arrested landings.[^cite_note-newhook-276] The increased wing area also allows for lower landing speeds and improves both range and payload capacity. The F-35C is limited to a maximum load factor of 7.5 g.[^cite_note-FY2019_President's_Budget_Selected_Acquisition_Report_(SAR)_–_F-35_Joint_Strike_Fighter_Aircraft_(F-35)-420]

### F-35I "Adir"

Main article: [Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II Israeli procurement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_Israeli_procurement)

The F-35I *Adir* ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): אדיר, meaning "Awesome",[^cite_note-421] or "Mighty One"[^cite_note-422]) is an F-35A with unique Israeli modifications. The US initially refused to allow such changes before permitting Israel to integrate its own electronic warfare systems, including sensors and countermeasures. The F-35I *Adir* is equipped with Israeli-made electronic warfare systems layered over the existing avionics and enhanced data gathering and processing capabilities. It comes with a specialized helmet designed for the aircraft's systems. While many of the details are classified, it is known that the helmet is equipped with a projector for each eye that feeds information directly to the pilots, eliminating the need to look down at cockpit instruments, and tracks the pilot's head movements to know where the pilot is looking as it relays data. The main computer has a plug-and-play function for add-on systems; proposals include an [external jamming pod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_jamming_and_deception), and new Israeli air-to-air missiles and guided bombs in the internal weapon bays. Due to unique component requirements, the F-35I's wings are locally manufactured.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424][^cite_note-Alon_Ben-David-425][^cite_note-AW_Israel_July2011-426] A senior IAF official said that the F-35's stealth may be partly overcome within 10 years despite a 30 to 40-year service life, thus Israel's insistence on using its own electronic warfare systems.[^cite_note-427] In 2010, [Israel Aerospace Industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Aerospace_Industries) (IAI) considered a two-seat F-35 concept; an IAI executive noted that there was a "known demand for two seats not only from Israel but from other air forces."[^cite_note-428] In 2008, IAI planned to produce [conformal fuel tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conformal_fuel_tank), as well as stealthy external fuel tanks;[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430] these were reportedly used during the [June 2025 Israeli airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2025_Israeli_strikes_on_Iran) against Iran.[^cite_note-auto2-384]

It is widely speculated that the F-35I may have the [capability to operate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_of_Israel#F-35I) as a [delivery vehicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_delivery) for [Israeli nuclear weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_nuclear_weapons), as it is a derivative of the nuclear-capable F-35A.[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432][^cite_note-k450-433] In 2018, Israeli [Channel 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_2_\(Israel\)) reported in 2014 that, following Lockheed Martin's nuclear-capable upgrades, allowing some F-35As' weapons systems and software to interface with US [permissive action links](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permissive_action_link), a senior US official had declined to comment on whether Israel had requested the same upgrade to its F-35Is.[^cite_note-f408-434]

### Proposed variant

#### CF-35

Main article: [Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II Canadian procurement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II_Canadian_procurement)

The Canadian CF-35 was a proposed variant that would differ from the F-35A through the addition of a [drogue parachute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drogue_parachute) and the potential inclusion of an F-35B/C-style refueling probe.[^cite_note-auto-414][^cite_note-C4Wauto-8740196-435] In 2012, it was revealed that the CF-35 would employ the same boom refueling system as the F-35A.[^cite_note-oCanada-2012-12-20-436] One alternative proposal would have been the adoption of the F-35C for its probe refueling and lower landing speed; however, the Parliamentary Budget Officer's report cited the F-35C's limited performance and payload as being too high a price to pay.[^cite_note-C4Wauto-2064727-437] Following the [2015 Federal Election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election), the [Liberal Party](149536.md), whose campaign had included a pledge to cancel the F-35 procurement,[^cite_note-Liberal2015Platform-438] formed a new government and commenced an open competition to replace the existing [CF-18 Hornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_CF-18_Hornet).[^cite_note-canada_exit-439] The CF-35 variant was deemed too expensive to develop, and was never considered. The Canadian government decided not to pursue any other modifications in the [Future Fighter Capability Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_Fighter_Capability_Project), and instead focused on the potential procurement of the existing F-35A variant.[^cite_note-440]

On 28 March 2022, the Canadian Government began negotiations with Lockheed Martin for 88 F-35As[^cite_note-Brewster_28Mar22-441] to replace the aging fleet of CF-18 fighters starting in 2025.[^cite_note-442] The aircraft are reported to cost up to CA\$19bn total with a life-cycle cost estimated at CA\$77bn over the course of the F-35 program.[^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444] On 9 January 2023, Canada formally confirmed the purchase of 88 aircraft. The initial delivery to the [Royal Canadian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Air_Force) in 2026 will be 4 aircraft, followed by 6 aircraft each in 2027–2028, and the rest to be delivered by 2032.[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446] The additional characteristics confirmed for Canadian F-35As included the drag chute pod for landings at short/icy arctic runways, as well as the 'sidekick' system, which allows the CF-35 to carry up to 6 x [AIM-120D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-120D) missiles internally (instead of the typical internal capacity of 4 x AIM-120 missiles on other variants).[^cite_note-447]

### New export variant

In December 2021, it was reported that Lockheed Martin was developing a new variant for an unspecified foreign customer. The Department of Defense released US\$49 million in funding for this work.[^cite_note-448]

### Future operators

 [Canada](5042916.md)

- [Royal Canadian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Air_Force) – 88 F-35As (Block 4) ordered on 9 January 2023. Canada has paid for 30 aircraft to be delivered between 2026 and 2029.

  &#x20;The plan was for the remainder to be delivered by 2032, but Canada decided in early 2025 to review the remaining 58 aircraft, possibly replacing them with [Saab Gripen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saab_Gripen). A preliminary audit indicated that the program's cost had escalated to \$27.7 billion from an initial \$19 billion.

  [^cite_note-519]

  [^cite_note-520]

### Potential operators

 [Morocco](19291.md)

- [Royal Moroccan Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Moroccan_Air_Force) – As of October 2025, Morocco has entered into negotiations with the United States for the acquisition of the F-35 Lightning II stealth multirole fighter for its air force. These discussions have been ongoing for several months, with documents indicating that Israel has granted its approval in principle for the sale. This authorization is provided within the framework of the [Qualitative Military Edge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qualitative_Military_Edge), a clause of the Arms Export Control Act requiring that no arms sale in the region compromise Israel's military superiority. If finalized, the agreement would make Morocco the first African and Arab nation to operate F-35s.

  [^cite_note-547]

 [Portugal](23033.md)

- [Portugal](23033.md) – The F-35 has been offered to Portugal to replace its ageing fleet of 28 F16 fighters. Other possible candidates include: [Eurofighter Typhoon](167667.md), [Gripen E](87577.md), and [Dassault Rafale](33731380.md).

  [^cite_note-548]

  [^cite_note-549]

 [Saudi Arabia](349303.md)

- [Royal Saudi Air Force](570367.md) – It was announced in November 2025 that Saudi Arabia would procure 48 F-35As. It is likely that the F-35As operated by the RSAF will lack some more advanced features found on Israel's fleet, to maintain the Qualitative Military Edge, with some American missiles not to be offered to Saudi Arabia.

  [^cite_note-550]

  [^cite_note-551]

  [^cite_note-552]

  &#x20;On 17 September 2026, the US Department of State announced that it had approved a potential F-35 sale to Saudi Arabia.

  [^cite_note-553]

### Cancelled operators

 [Republic of China](25734.md)

- [Republic of China Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_Air_Force) – Taiwan has repeatedly expressed interest in buying the F-35 to deter and fight off any Chinese attempt to seize the island by force. It is reportedly most interested in the F-35B STOVL variant, which could enable the Republic of China Air Force to continue operations if China bombed the island's runways.

  [^cite_note-554]

  &#x20;But the US has repeatedly rebuffed this interest

  [^cite_note-555]

  &#x20;– for example, in March 2009, September 2011,

  [^cite_note-556]

  &#x20;early 2017

  [^cite_note-557]

  &#x20;and March 2018. The usual reason given is to prevent provoking Beijing. But in April 2018, another reason for US reluctance surfaced: concern that Chinese spies within the [Taiwanese Armed Forces](30095.md) might gain classified data about the aircraft. In November 2018, it was reported that Taiwanese military leaders had abandoned efforts to buy the F-35 and would instead buy [Rafale](33731380.md)&#x20;

  [^cite_note-558]

  &#x20;or a larger number of [F-16V Viper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_F-16V_Viper) aircraft. The decision was reportedly motivated by concerns about industry independence, cost, and espionage.

  [^cite_note-559]

 [Egypt](8087628.md)

- [Egyptian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Air_Force) – Despite US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s announcement to sell 20 F-35As to Egypt in 2018, strong objections from the US Department of Defense and [Israel](9282173.md) led to the deal's cancellation.

  [^cite_note-560]

 [India](14533.md)

- [Indian Air Force](403355.md) – In February 2025, Trump offered the F-35 to [Prime Minister](24452.md) [Narendra Modi](444222.md) of India,

  [^cite_note-561]

  &#x20;which as of March 2025, was also considering a competing offer from Russia's [Sukhoi Su-57](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-57).

  [^cite_note-562]

  &#x20;However, in late July 2025, *[Bloomberg](3057255.md)* reported that India has already rejected its requirement of the aircraft. While the [Indian Ministry of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(India\)) has not confirmed any of this, the [Indian Ministry of External Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ministry_of_External_Affairs) maintained that India has not held any formal discussions about the aircraft's procurement with the US.

  [^cite_note-563]

  [^cite_note-564]

 [Spain](26667.md)

- [Spanish Air and Space Force](3853398.md) – The Spanish government explored the acquisition of the F-35 through requests for information and preliminary analysis,

  [^cite_note-565]

  &#x20;but in August 2025 the option was formally ruled out.

  [^cite_note-566]

 [Thailand](30128.md)

- [Royal Thai Air Force](1778758.md) – 8 or 12 F-35s are planned to replace the [F-16A/B Block 15 ADFs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics_F-16_Fighting_Falcon_variants#F-16A/B_Block_15_ADF) in service. On 12 January 2022, Thailand's cabinet approved a budget for the first four F-35As, estimated at 13.8 billion baht in FY2023.

  [^cite_note-567]

  [^cite_note-568]

  [^cite_note-569]

  &#x20;On 22 May 2023, the [United States Department of Defense](7279897.md) implied it will turn down Thailand's bid to buy F-35s, and instead offer [F-16 Block 70/72 Viper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics_F-16_Fighting_Falcon_variants#F-16_Block_70/72) and [F-15EX Eagle II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_F-15EX_Eagle_II) fighters, a Royal Thai Air Force source said.

  [^cite_note-570]

 [Turkey](11125639.md)

- On 11 July 2002, Turkey became a Level 3 partner of the [F-35 Joint Strike Fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-35_Joint_Strike_Fighter) (JSF) development program, and on 25 January 2007, Turkey officially joined the production phase of the JSF program, agreeing to purchase a total of 116 [F-35 Lightning II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-35_Lightning_II) aircraft (100 [F-35A](#F-35A) [CTOLs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CTOL) for the [Turkish Air Force](1799517.md) and 16 [F-35B](#F-35B) [STOVLs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STOVL) for the [TCG Anadolu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TCG_Anadolu)).

  [^cite_note-571]

  &#x20;Future purchases have been banned by the US following Turkey's decision to buy the [S-400 missile system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-400_missile_system) from Russia, with contracts canceled by early 2020.

  [^cite_note-turkeyban-572]

  &#x20;Six of Turkey's 30 ordered F-35As were completed as of 2019 (they are still kept in a hangar in the United States as of 2023

  [^cite_note-FlightGlobal-23.01.2023-573]

  [^cite_note-Janes-24.01.2023-574]

  &#x20;and so far haven't been transferred to the USAF, despite a modification in the 2020 fiscal year defense budget by the US Congress which gives authority to do so if necessary),

  [^cite_note-TRT_World-21.07.2020-575]

  [^cite_note-DefenseNews-20.07.2020-576]

  &#x20;and two more were at the assembly line in 2020.

  [^cite_note-TRT_World-21.07.2020-575]

  [^cite_note-DefenseNews-20.07.2020-576]

  &#x20;The first four F-35As were delivered to [Luke Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luke_Air_Force_Base) in 2018

  [^cite_note-577]

  &#x20;and 2019

  [^cite_note-578]

  &#x20;for the training of Turkish pilots.

  [^cite_note-579]

  [^cite_note-580]

  &#x20;On 20 July 2020, the US government had formally approved the seizure of eight F-35As originally bound for Turkey and their transfer to the USAF, together with a contract to modify them to USAF specifications.

  [^cite_note-581]

  &#x20;The US has not refunded the \$1.4 billion payment made by Turkey for purchasing the F-35As as of January 2023.

  [^cite_note-FlightGlobal-23.01.2023-573]

  [^cite_note-Janes-24.01.2023-574]

  &#x20;On 1 February 2024, the United States expressed willingness to readmit Turkey into the F-35 program if Turkey agrees to give up its S-400 system.

  [^cite_note-582]

  &#x20;After US President Trump and Turkish President Erdoğan's phone call in March 2025, news was reported in the press that Trump could approve the sale of F-35s to Turkey if Turkey resolves the S-400 issue.

  [^cite_note-583]

  &#x20;Following a meeting between Trump and Erdoğan at the White House on September 25, 2025, it was revealed that one of the proposed solutions, reported by the media in October 2025, stipulates the relocation of Turkey's S-400 missile systems to the [Nakhchivan Autonomous Republic](385358.md), an exclave of [Azerbaijan](746.md) which has a five-mile (8.0 km) border with Turkey, as a precondition for lifting the [Countering America's Adversaries Through Sanctions Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countering_America's_Adversaries_Through_Sanctions_Act#Turkey) (CAATSA) sanctions and approving the sale and transfer of F-35s and [Patriot](52024.md) surface-to-air missile systems to Turkey.

  [^cite_note-584]

  &#x20;On 24 June 2026, during a joint press conference with NATO Secretary General [Mark Rutte](4321056.md) at the White House, Trump stated that he would "probably do something that will make Turkey very happy" regarding Turkey's request to rejoin the F-35 program, ahead of the [NATO Summit](80796458.md) in Ankara.

  [^cite_note-585]

  &#x20;The same day, Reuters reported that the US administration was expected to approve the export of [General Electric F110](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric_F110) engines for Turkey's [KAAN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TAI_TF_Kaan) fighter program before the summit, despite opposition in US Congress.

  [^cite_note-586]

  [^cite_note-587]

  &#x20;Turkey's Ministry of National Defense announced that diplomatic consultations with US counterparts on the country's potential return to the F-35 program and the removal of related restrictions were continuing. US officials, however, emphasized that any policy shift would depend on addressing concerns regarding Turkey's possession of the Russian [S-400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-400) air defense system.

  [^cite_note-588]

  [^cite_note-589]

  [^cite_note-590]

 [United Arab Emirates](69328.md)

- [United Arab Emirates Air Force](10815770.md) – Up to 50 F-35As planned.

  [^cite_note-591]

  &#x20;On 27 January 2021, the Biden administration temporarily suspended the F-35 sales to the UAE.

  [^cite_note-592]

  &#x20;After pausing the bill to review the sale, the Biden administration confirmed it would move forward with the deal on 13 April 2021.

  [^cite_note-593]

  &#x20;In December 2021, the UAE withdrew from purchasing F-35s as they did not agree to the additional terms of the transaction from the US.

  [^cite_note-594]

  [^cite_note-595]

  &#x20;On 14 September 2024, a senior UAE official said that the UAE does not expect to resume talks with the US about the F-35.

  [^cite_note-596]

## Accidents and notable incidents

Main article: [List of accidents and incidents involving the Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_accidents_and_incidents_involving_the_Lockheed_Martin_F-35_Lightning_II)

The F-35 is a statistically safe military aircraft, described as being twice as safe as the F-16.[^cite_note-597] Still, since 2014, more than a dozen have crashed or otherwise been involved in incidents that have killed or severely injured people or destroyed the aircraft. Some were caused by operator error; others by mechanical problems, some of which set the entire program back.[^cite_note-598]

As of August 2026, there were 12 crashes causing the loss of an F-35 airframe, primarily in the US.[^cite_note-\:20-404]

The only known fatal crash of an F-35 occurred on 9 April 2019, when a [Japan Air Self-Defense Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Air_Self-Defense_Force) F-35A crashed in the Pacific Ocean east of [Aomori Prefecture](61732.md) at an estimated speed of Mach 0.9.[^cite_note-\:20-404] The accident report attributed the cause to the pilot's [spatial disorientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spatial_disorientation). The pilot's remains and the flight recorder were salvaged, although no data could be retrieved, while the wreckage itself was not salvaged.[^cite_note-spatial_disorientation-401]

On 31 July 2026, a US Marine Corps F-35B crashed and was destroyed at [Marine Corps Air Station Miramar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Corps_Air_Station_Miramar) in San Diego, California. The pilot ejected safely.[^cite_note-599][^cite_note-600][^cite_note-601]

### 2026 Iran war

On 19 March 2026, during the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md), a US F-35 flying a combat mission over Iran was forced to make an emergency landing at a regional air base.[^cite_note-\:13-602] The [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md) said it had "seriously damaged" the F-35.[^cite_note-\:14-603][^cite_note-\:15-604] It was the first time an F-35 sustained damage from enemy fire since it was deployed in combat in 2018.[^cite_note-605] It was also the first Iranian interception of any US aircraft.[^cite_note-\:16-606] The aircraft landed safely.[^cite_note-\:13-602]

On 15 September 2026, the [Congressional Budget Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office) published the report "Estimating the Cost of Combat Operations Against Iran", which listed an F-35A "battle loss" which it estimated could be repaired for \$50 million or replaced for the original unit cost of \$120 million.[^cite_note-607]

## UK F-35 exports to Israel and legal challenge

The United Kingdom's participation in the F-35 program became the subject of political and legal controversy during the [Gaza war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Gaza_War). In September 2024, the British government suspended 29 arms export licenses after concluding that there was a clear risk that the items concerned might be used to commit or facilitate serious violations of international humanitarian law in Gaza. Licenses supplying components to the multinational F-35 program were exempted where the government could not identify Israel as the final end user. Ministers argued that suspending all UK supplies to the program would disrupt the global F-35 supply chain and have serious consequences for the defense of the United Kingdom, NATO allies and other international partners.[^cite_note-608]

The Palestinian human rights organisation [Al-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Haq) challenged the F-35 exemption in judicial review proceedings. In June 2025, the [UK High Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_High_Court) dismissed the challenge. The court noted that the structure of the multinational program meant that F-35 aircraft supplied to Israel would contain UK-manufactured components, but held that the central question involved the government's continued participation in a multilateral defense program regarded by ministers as vital to national and international security. It concluded that this "acutely sensitive and political issue" was for the executive, accountable to Parliament and the electorate, rather than the courts.[^cite_note-609]

Al-Haq sought to appeal the ruling, arguing that the exemption was incompatible with the UK's international law obligations and its own arms-export controls. In November 2025, the [Court of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Appeal_\(England_and_Wales\)) refused permission for a further appeal, leaving the F-35 exemption in place.[^cite_note-610]

[^cite_note-281]: &#x20;"Bubbling and blistering" of the horizontal tails and tail booms were observed once during flutter tests of the F-35B and C in late 2011; according to the program office, the problem has only occurred once despite numerous attempts to replicate it, and an improved spray-on coating has been implemented since as a mitigation measure. On 17 December 2019, the Pentagon program office closed the issue with no further actions planned, and instead is imposing a time limit on high-speed flight for the F-35B and C to reduce the risk of damaging the stealth coatings and antennas located on the back of the aircraft.<sup>[\[264\]](#cite_note-279)</sup><sup>[\[265\]](#cite_note-280)</sup>

[^cite_note-284]: &#x20;Wing drop is an uncommanded roll that can occur during high-g transsonic maneuvering.

[^cite_note-619]: &#x20;F-35B and F-35C have the cannon in an external pod with 220 rounds.
